e_{y}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}
.
If we now represent subsets (events)
A\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq Y
by their corresponding indicator functions
\chi_{A}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
and
\chi_{B}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}
, respectively, we see that the Bayes condition gives

\xi\big(G(\chi_{A})\chi_{B}\big)=\omega\big(\chi_{A}F(\chi_{B})\big).

(3.34)

It is helpful to evaluate these expressions more explicitly. We obtain

G\left(\chi_{A}\right)=\sum_{x\in A\subseteq X}G(e_{x})=\sum_{x\in A\subseteq X}\sum_{y\in Y}g_{xy}e_{y},

(3.35)

while

F\left(\chi_{B}\right)=\sum_{y\in B\subseteq Y}F(e_{y})=\sum_{y\in B\subseteq Y}\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}e_{x}.

(3.36)

Hence,

\xi\big(G(\chi_{A})\chi_{B}\big)=\sum_{x\in A\subseteq X}\sum_{y\in Y}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in B\subseteq Y}g_{xy}\xi\left(e_{y}e_{y^{\prime}}\right)=\sum_{x\in A\subseteq X}\sum_{y\in B\subseteq Y}g_{xy}\xi(e_{y})=\sum_{y\in B\subseteq Y}g_{y}(A)q_{y}

(3.37)

while

\omega\big(\chi_{A}F(\chi_{B})\big)=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in A\subseteq X}\sum_{y\in B\subseteq Y}\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}\omega\left(e_{x^{\prime}}e_{x}\right)=\sum_{x\in A\subseteq X}\sum_{y\in B\subseteq Y}f_{yx}\omega(e_{x})=\sum_{x\in A\subseteq X}f_{x}(B)p_{x}

(3.38)

The equality of these two expressions is precisely Bayes’ Theorem mentioned earlier in Remark
2.29
.

We will provide more examples of when Bayesian inverses exist, but first we
provide some lemmas that will be useful later.

Lemma 3.39
.

If
F:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a CPU map between
C^{*}
-algebras, and if
(F,\omega)
admits a Bayesian inverse
G
, then given any unitaries
U\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
V\in{{\mathcal{B}}}
, the CPU map
\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{{\dagger}}}\circ G\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}
is a Bayesian inverse of
(\mathrm{Ad}_{U}\circ F\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{V},\omega\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}})
. Conversely, if
\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{{\dagger}}}\circ G\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}
is a Bayesian inverse of
(\mathrm{Ad}_{U}\circ F\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{V},\omega\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}})
, then
G
is a Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega
).

Remark 3.40
.

In the notation of Lemma
3.39
, if we set
\xi:=\omega\circ F
, we can depict these data diagrammatically as

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\omega\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}}
\scriptstyle{\xi\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}}
\scriptstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{{\dagger}}}}

(3.41)

to help the reader visualize all the maps involved. In particular, when
{{\mathcal{B}}}=\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}=\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
, since the states
\omega=:{\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)
and
\xi=:{\rm tr}(\sigma\;\cdot\;)
can be described by density matrices, this means that we can find unitary matrices
U
and
V
that diagonalize these density matrices and find the Bayesian inverse of the associated maps after diagonalization.
When
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
are direct sums of matrix algebras, the unitaries act componentwise on the factors.

Lemma 3.42
.

Set
{{\mathcal{A}}}:=\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
. Then

\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}(E_{ik})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}E_{ij}\otimes E_{jk},

(3.43)

where the
E_{ij}
are the matrix units and
\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}
denotes the dual of the multiplication map
\mu_{A}:{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
with respect to the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product.

Proof.

We set
\alpha_{ghnopq}
to be the coefficients appearing in

\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}(E_{gh})=:\sum_{n,o,p,q}\alpha_{ghnopq}E_{no}\otimes E_{pq}.

(3.44)

We also let
\langle\;\cdot\;,\;\cdot\;\rangle
denote the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product
(both on the tensor product and on the original algebra).
Then we have

\textstyle{\langle\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}(E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl}),E_{gh}\rangle}
\textstyle{\delta_{jk}\langle E_{il},E_{gh}\rangle}
\textstyle{\delta_{jk}{\rm tr}(E_{il}^{{\dagger}}E_{gh})}
\textstyle{\delta_{jk}\delta_{ig}\delta_{lh}}
\textstyle{\alpha_{ghijkl}}
\textstyle{\sum\limits_{n,o,p,q}\alpha_{ghnopq}\delta_{in}\delta_{jo}\delta_{kp}\delta_{lq}}
\textstyle{\sum\limits_{n,o,p,q}\alpha_{ghnopq}{\rm tr}(E_{ij}^{{\dagger}}E_{no}){\rm tr}(E_{kl}^{{\dagger}}E_{pq})}
\textstyle{\sum\limits_{n,o,p,q}\langle E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl},\alpha_{ghnopq}E_{no}\otimes E_{pq}\rangle}
\textstyle{\langle E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl},\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}(E_{gh})\rangle}

(3.45)

Therefore,

\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}(E_{gh})=\sum_{i,j,k,l}\alpha_{ghijkl}E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}\delta_{jk}\delta_{ig}\delta_{lh}E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl}=\sum_{j}E_{gj}\otimes E_{jh}

(3.46)

as needed.
∎

Remark 3.47
.

The map
\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}
is the co-multiplication map of the Frobenius structure on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
as described in Section 3.5 of Coecke and Spekkens
[
10
]
.
Since
\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
is in general not completely positive, neither is
\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}
(recall, a map on finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras is CP if and only if its dual with respect to the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product is CP).

Lemma 3.48
.

Let
F:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a linear map, let
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state, and set
\xi:=\omega\circ F
. If
F
is positive unital, then

F\big(P_{\xi}^{\perp}\mathcal{B}P_{\xi}^{\perp}\big)\subseteq P_{\omega}^{\perp}\mathcal{A}P_{\omega}^{\perp}.

(3.49)

If
F
is 2-positive, then

F(\mathcal{N}_{\xi})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\omega},

(3.50)

Proof.

Lemma 3.121 in
[
40
]
is the first statement and Proposition 3.106 from
[
40
]
is almost the second statement. Technically, Proposition 3.106 from
[
40
]
assumed
F
was 2-positive unital, but 2-positivity is enough because in this case, the Kadison–Cauchy–Schwarz inequality reads
F(B)^{*}F(B)\leq\lVert F(1_{{{\mathcal{B}}}})\rVert F(B^{*}B)
for all
B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}
.
∎

Remark 3.51
.

Condition (
3.50
) is not generally true if
F
is only positive unital. For example, take
F
to be the transpose map on
2\times 2
matrices and
\rho=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right]
with
\omega={\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)=\xi
.

4
Examples of quantum Bayesian inference

Before proving general existence and uniqueness theorems for quantum Bayesian inference, we examine several special cases and their physical relevance. These examples will also supply additional intuition for the more general results that will come later.

4.1
The bit flip channel

Set

{{\mathcal{A}}}:=\mathcal{M}_{2}({{\mathbb{C}}}),{{\mathcal{B}}}:=\mathcal{M}_{2}({{\mathbb{C}}}),\rho=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}p_{1}&0\\
0&p_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right],

and

F:=\lambda_{1}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{2}({{\mathbb{C}}})}+\lambda_{2}\mathrm{Ad}_{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right]},

where
0\leq p_{1},p_{2}\leq 1,

0<\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}<1
, and
p_{1}+p_{2}=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
(the reason for choosing
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(0,1)
is because if one of them is
0
then
F
is
\mathrm{Ad}_{U}
with
U
unitary, which is invertible, and hence has a Bayesian inverse, namely
\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{\dagger}}
).
The map
F
here is called the
bit flip channel

[
37
, Section 8.3.3]
.
Then

\sigma:=F^{*}(\rho)=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}p_{1}+\lambda_{2}p_{2}&0\\
0&\lambda_{1}p_{2}+\lambda_{2}p_{1}\end{bmatrix}=:\begin{bmatrix}q_{1}&0\\
0&q_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

(4.1)

Hence,
F
pulls back the state
\omega:={\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)
to
\xi:={\rm tr}(\sigma\;\cdot\;)
. In what follows, we will discover when a Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega)
exists.
First note that
F^{*}=F
and

F(E_{ij})=\begin{cases}\lambda_{1}E_{ij}+\lambda_{2}E_{ji}&\mbox{ if $i\neq j$}\\
\lambda_{1}E_{11}+\lambda_{2}E_{22}&\mbox{ if $i=j=1$}\\
\lambda_{1}E_{22}+\lambda_{2}E_{11}&\mbox{ if $i=j=2$}\\
\end{cases}.

(4.2)

The unital linear functional
\zeta:=\omega\circ\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes F)
, the right side of the rectangle in (
3.27
), is uniquely determined by the matrix

\begin{split}\tau&:=\zeta^{*}(1)=(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes F^{*})\Big(\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}\big(\underbrace{\omega^{*}(1)}_{\rho}\big)\Big)=(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes F^{*})\big(\mu_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}^{*}(p_{1}E_{11}+p_{2}E_{22})\big)\\
&\overset{\text{Lem~\ref{lem:comultiplication}}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes F^{*})(p_{1}E_{11}\otimes E_{11}+p_{1}E_{12}\otimes E_{21}+p_{2}E_{21}\otimes E_{12}+p_{2}E_{22}\otimes E_{22})\\
&=p_{1}E_{11}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{bmatrix}+p_{1}E_{12}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}0&\lambda_{2}\\
\lambda_{1}&0\end{bmatrix}+p_{2}E_{21}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}0&\lambda_{1}\\
\lambda_{2}&0\end{bmatrix}+p_{2}E_{22}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{2}&0\\
0&\lambda_{1}\end{bmatrix},\end{split}

(4.3)

which simplifies to
8
8

8

Note that
\tau
is not a density matrix unless
p_{1}=p_{2}
. Nevertheless, we do not in general expect
\tau
to be a density matrix (see Remark
5.96
for further discussion). Hence, we do not impose that
\tau
need to be a density matrix in what follows.

\tau=\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}\lambda_{1}&0&0&p_{1}\lambda_{2}\\
0&p_{1}\lambda_{2}&p_{1}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&p_{2}\lambda_{1}&p_{2}\lambda_{2}&0\\
p_{2}\lambda_{2}&0&0&p_{2}\lambda_{1}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

(4.4)

A Bayesian inverse
G
of
F
must satisfy

\tau=(G^{*}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{B}}}})\big(\mu_{{{\mathcal{B}}}}^{*}(\sigma)\big),

(4.5)

because this reproduces the left part of the rectangle in (
3.27
) upon taking the dual.
The general form of a CP map
G
is

G=\sum_{j}\mathrm{Ad}_{G_{j}},\qquad\text{ where }\qquad G_{j}=\begin{bmatrix}g_{j;11}&g_{j;12}\\
g_{j;21}&g_{j;22}\end{bmatrix}.

(4.6)

Therefore,

\begin{split}(G^{*}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{B}}}})\big(\mu_{{{\mathcal{B}}}}^{*}(\sigma)\big)&=(G^{*}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{B}}}})\big(q_{1}E_{11}\otimes E_{11}+q_{1}E_{12}\otimes E_{21}+q_{2}E_{21}\otimes E_{12}+q_{2}E_{22}\otimes E_{22}\big)\\
&=\sum_{j}\begin{bmatrix}[1.3]q_{1}|g_{j;11}|^{2}&q_{2}\overline{g_{j;21}}g_{j;11}&q_{1}\overline{g_{j;11}}g_{j;12}&q_{2}\overline{g_{j;21}}g_{j;12}\\
q_{1}\overline{g_{j;11}}g_{j;21}&q_{2}|g_{j;21}|^{2}&q_{1}\overline{g_{j;11}}g_{j;22}&q_{2}\overline{g_{j;21}}g_{j;22}\\
q_{1}\overline{g_{j;12}}g_{j;11}&q_{2}\overline{g_{j;22}}g_{j;11}&q_{1}|g_{j;12}|^{2}&q_{2}\overline{g_{j;22}}g_{j;12}\\
q_{1}\overline{g_{j;12}}g_{j;21}&q_{2}\overline{g_{j;22}}g_{j;21}&q_{1}\overline{g_{j;12}}g_{j;22}&q_{2}|g_{j;22}|^{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

(4.7)

Equating this with
\tau
in (
4.4
) gives several relations between the
p
’s and
\lambda
’s.
In particular, the non-vanishing off-diagonal terms give

\begin{split}p_{1}\lambda_{2}&=q_{2}\sum_{j}\overline{g_{j;21}}g_{j;12},\quad p_{1}\lambda_{1}=q_{1}\sum_{j}\overline{g_{j;11}}g_{j;22},\quad\\
p_{2}\lambda_{1}&=q_{2}\sum_{j}\overline{g_{j;22}}g_{j;11},\quad p_{2}\lambda_{2}=q_{1}\sum_{j}\overline{g_{j;12}}g_{j;21}.\end{split}

(4.8)

Multiplying the left equations by
q_{1}
and the right equations by
q_{2}
gives

p_{1}\lambda_{2}q_{1}=q_{2}q_{1}\sum_{j}\overline{g_{j;21}}g_{j;12}=q_{2}q_{1}\sum_{j}\overline{g_{j;12}}g_{j;21}=q_{2}p_{2}\lambda_{2}.

(4.9)

since the equations (
4.8
) are invariant under complex conjugation. Since
\lambda_{2}\neq 0,
this implies
p_{1}q_{1}=p_{2}q_{2}
. Expanding this out using the definition of the
q
’s gives
\lambda_{1}p_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}p_{1}p_{2}=\lambda_{1}p_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{2}p_{1}p_{2}
, which entails
p_{1}=p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore, a necessary condition for a Bayesian inverse to exist is that
p_{1}=p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
, which entails
q_{1}=q_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
. In this case,
G=F
is such a Bayesian inverse as can be checked.

Remark 4.10
.

It is a curious fact that Bayesian inference does not exist in the simple situation covered in this example of the bit flip map. This is particularly surprising since a classical analogue
9
9

9

This is not an analogue in the rigorous sense of category theory but only in the sense of what some may consider a classical analogue based on the fact that we have used diagonal density matrices. Such analogies have been shown to be misleading in other cases as well and have led to the discovery of concepts such as the quantum discord
[
38
]
.

of this situation may be considered to be the following. If
\mathcal{M}_{2}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is replaced with
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{2},
the states
\omega
and
\xi
are replaced by the (dual) probability vectors
\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}&p_{2}\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}q_{1}&q_{2}\end{bmatrix}
, and
F
is the (doubly) stochastic matrix given by
F=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\lambda_{2}\\
\lambda_{2}&\lambda_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, then the relationships
q_{1}=\lambda_{1}p_{1}+\lambda_{2}p_{2}
and
q_{2}=\lambda_{2}p_{1}+\lambda_{1}p_{2}
still hold. Furthermore, a Bayesian inference exists regardless of the values of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Namely,

G=\begin{bmatrix}[1.3]p_{1}\lambda_{1}/q_{1}&p_{2}\lambda_{2}/q_{1}\\
p_{1}\lambda_{2}/q_{2}&p_{2}\lambda_{1}/q_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

(4.11)

Note that
q_{1},q_{2}\in(0,1)
because
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(0,1)
.

4.2
Positive operator-valued measures

Definition 4.12
.

A
positive operator-valued measure
(POVM) on the Hilbert space
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
is a collection of positive operators
\{F_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
in
\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
, where
Y
is a finite set and
\sum\limits_{y\in Y}F_{y}=\mathds{1}_{m}
. Equivalently, these data are described by a (necessarily completely) positive unital map
F:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
, since one can identify
F_{y}
with
F(e_{y}),
where
e_{y}
is the function on
Y
whose value at
y
is
1
and is zero everywhere else.
If
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebra, an
operator system
in
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a (complex) vector subspace
\mathcal{O}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
that contains
1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
and is closed under the involution of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
If
{{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrel{\hbox to15.92pt{\vbox to11.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.96034pt\lower-6.79025pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.40758pt}{-0.8264pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.18 -6.28 L 9.35 -6.28 C 8.32 -6.28 7.8 -7.46 6.77 -7.46 C 6.02 -7.46 5.38 -6.89 4.7 -6.28 C 4.03 -5.68 3.39 -5.1 2.64 -5.1 C 1.89 -5.1 1.24 -5.68 0.57 -6.28 C -0.1 -6.89 -0.75 -7.46 -1.5 -7.46 C -2.25 -7.46 -2.89 -6.89 -3.56 -6.28 C -4.24 -5.68 -4.88 -5.1 -5.63 -5.1 L -10.74 -6.28 L -10.74 -6.28}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.56035pt}{-4.54025pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a linear map, the
fixed point set
of
\varphi
is the vector subspace of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
given by

\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi):=\big\{A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\;:\;\varphi(A)=A\big\}.

(4.13)

If
{{\mathcal{A}}}=\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is a matrix algebra and
\varphi=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{\alpha}}
is a Kraus decomposition of
\varphi
, the
interaction algebra
associated with the Kraus operators
\{V_{\alpha}\}
is given by

\mathrm{Alg}(\{V_{\alpha}\}),

the algebra generated by the Kraus operators, i.e. all polynomials in the
\{V_{\alpha}\}
.

We recall some important facts due to Kribs
[
30
]
(see also
[
20
, Remark 2.1 and Theorem 3.4]
).

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\mathrel{\hbox to15.92pt{\vbox to11.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.96034pt\lower-6.79025pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.18 -6.28 L 9.35 -6.28 C 8.32 -6.28 7.8 -7.46 6.77 -7.46 C 6.02 -7.46 5.38 -6.89 4.7 -6.28 C 4.03 -5.68 3.39 -5.1 2.64 -5.1 C 1.89 -5.1 1.24 -5.68 0.57 -6.28 C -0.1 -6.89 -0.75 -7.46 -1.5 -7.46 C -2.25 -7.46 -2.89 -6.89 -3.56 -6.28 C -4.24 -5.68 -4.88 -5.1 -5.63 -5.1 L -10.74 -6.28 L -10.74 -6.28}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.56035pt}{-4.54025pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a completely positive unital and trace-preserving map with a Kraus decomposition
\varphi=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{\alpha}}
. Then

i.

\mathrm{Alg}(\{V_{\alpha}\})
is a (unital)
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and depends only on the map
\varphi
and not the specific choice of Kraus operators (as such, it will henceforth be denoted by
\mathrm{Alg}(\varphi)
).

ii.

The fixed point set of
\varphi
is a (unital)
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and equals the commutant of the interaction algebra, i.e.

\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Alg}(\varphi)^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(4.15)

The equivalent formulation of a POVM as a CPU map allows us to ask when a POVM has a Bayesian inverse. For this, we have a thorough and detailed result given by Proposition
4.19
below.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
A_{ij}
denote the
ij
-th entry of
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
. Then
A_{ij}={\rm tr}(AE_{ji})
.
Furthermore, if
F:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is a linear map, then
F
is
*
-preserving if and only if
F^{*}
, the Hilbert–Schmit dual of
F
, is
*
-preserving.

Proof.

Using Dirac bra-ket notation gives

{\rm tr}(AE_{ji})=\sum_{k}\langle k|AE_{ji}|k\rangle=\sum_{k}\langle k|A|j\rangle\langle i|k\rangle=\sum_{k}\langle i|k\rangle\langle k|A|j\rangle=\langle i|A|j\rangle=A_{ij},

(4.17)

where the completeness relation
\sum_{k=1}^{m}|k\rangle\langle k|=\mathds{1}_{m}
has been used in the fourth step.
The second claim follows from the first. Namely, if
F
is
*
-preserving, then

\begin{split}\big(F^{*}(A^{\dagger})\big)_{ij}&={\rm tr}\big(F^{*}(A^{\dagger})E_{ji}\big)={\rm tr}\big(A^{\dagger}F(E_{ji})\big)=\overline{{\rm tr}\big(F(E_{ji})^{\dagger}A\big)}\\
&=\overline{{\rm tr}\big(F(E_{ij})A\big)}=\overline{{\rm tr}\big(E_{ij}F^{*}(A)\big)}={\rm tr}\big(F^{*}(A)^{{\dagger}}E_{ji}\big)=\big(F^{*}(A)^{{\dagger}}\big)_{ij}\end{split}

(4.18)

by cyclicity of trace, the
*
-preserving property of trace, and the
*
-preserving property of
F
. A similar calculation proves the converse.
∎

{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.8 -6.56 L 9.97 -6.56 C 8.94 -6.56 8.42 -7.75 7.39 -7.75 C 6.64 -7.75 5.99 -7.17 5.32 -6.56 C 4.64 -5.96 4 -5.38 3.25 -5.38 C 2.5 -5.38 1.86 -5.96 1.18 -6.56 C 0.51 -7.17 -0.13 -7.75 -0.88 -7.75 C -1.63 -7.75 -2.28 -7.17 -2.95 -6.56 C -3.62 -5.96 -4.27 -5.38 -5.02 -5.38 L -11.35 -6.56 L -11.35 -6.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.00446pt}{-4.74443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a state-preserving POVM. Set
F_{y}:=F(e_{y})
and
q_{y}:={\rm tr}(\rho F_{y})
for each
y\in Y
. Then the following are equivalent.

i.

A Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega)
exists.

ii.

The commutation rule

[\rho,F_{y}]=0\quad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q}

(4.20)

holds.

iii.

The density matrix
\rho
is in the commutant of the algebra generated by the operator system

\mathrm{span}\{F_{y}\;:\;y\in Y\},\qquad\text{ i.e.\ }\qquad\rho\in F\left({{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\right)^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(4.21)

iv.

The density matrix
\rho
satisfies

\sum_{y\in Y}\sqrt{F_{y}}\rho\sqrt{F_{y}}=\rho,

(4.22)

i.e.
\rho
is in the fixed point set for the CP map
\sum_{y\in Y}\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{F_{y}}}
.

When one (and hence all) of these conditions is satisfied, a Bayesian inverse is
\xi
-a.e. uniquely determined and a representative is given by the formula

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})&\mathrel{\hbox to16.89pt{\vbox to11.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.44347pt\lower-6.99443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.8907pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.85 -6.56 L 10.02 -6.56 C 8.99 -6.56 8.47 -7.75 7.44 -7.75 C 6.69 -7.75 6.05 -7.17 5.37 -6.56 C 4.7 -5.96 4.05 -5.38 3.31 -5.38 C 2.56 -5.38 1.91 -5.96 1.24 -6.56 C 0.56 -7.17 -0.08 -7.75 -0.83 -7.75 C -1.58 -7.75 -2.22 -7.17 -2.9 -6.56 C -3.57 -5.96 -4.21 -5.38 -4.96 -5.38 L -11.41 -6.56 L -11.41 -6.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.04347pt}{-4.74443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\\
A&\mapsto G(A):=\sum_{y\in Y\setminus N_{q}}\frac{{\rm tr}(\rho AF_{y})}{q_{y}}e_{y}+\sum_{y\in N_{q}}\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(A)e_{y}.\end{split}

(4.23)

Proof.

Set
{{\mathcal{A}}}:=\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}:={{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}
.
Note that
\xi(e_{y})=q_{y}
for all
y\in Y
.
Hence, for
y\in N_{q}
, which means
e_{y}\in P_{\xi}^{\perp}\mathcal{B}P_{\xi}^{\perp}
, Lemma
3.48

implies
F_{y}\in P_{\omega}^{\perp}\mathcal{A}P_{\omega}^{\perp}
. Hence,
F_{y}\rho=0
for
y\in N_{q}
and therefore
{\rm tr}(\rho AF_{y})=0=q_{y}G(A)_{y}
so that the Bayes condition holds automatically for all
y\in N_{q}
for any linear map
{{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrel{\hbox to16.89pt{\vbox to11.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.44347pt\lower-6.99443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.8907pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.85 -6.56 L 10.02 -6.56 C 8.99 -6.56 8.47 -7.75 7.44 -7.75 C 6.69 -7.75 6.05 -7.17 5.37 -6.56 C 4.7 -5.96 4.05 -5.38 3.31 -5.38 C 2.56 -5.38 1.91 -5.96 1.24 -6.56 C 0.56 -7.17 -0.08 -7.75 -0.83 -7.75 C -1.58 -7.75 -2.22 -7.17 -2.9 -6.56 C -3.57 -5.96 -4.21 -5.38 -4.96 -5.38 L -11.41 -6.56 L -11.41 -6.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.04347pt}{-4.74443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathcal{B}}}
. Here,
G(A)_{y}:=(\mathrm{ev}_{y}\circ G)(A)
denotes the
y
-th component of
G(A)
.

(
i
\Rightarrow
ii
)
Suppose a Bayesian inverse
G
exists. The Bayes condition entails
{\rm tr}(\rho AF_{y})=\xi\big(G(A)e_{y}\big)=q_{y}G(A)_{y}
for all
y\in Y
and for all
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Thus,

G(A)_{y}=\frac{{\rm tr}(\rho AF_{y})}{q_{y}}\qquad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q},\;\;\forall\;A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

(4.24)

Since
G
is CP, its
y
component
\mathrm{ev}_{y}\circ G
is also CP. In particular,
\mathrm{ev}_{y}\circ G
is
*
-preserving, which means
\overline{G(A^{\dagger})_{y}}=G(A)_{y}
for all
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
. This implies

{\rm tr}(\rho AF_{y})={\rm tr}(F_{y}A\rho)\qquad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q},\;\;\forall\;A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}

(4.25)

because the trace is
*
-preserving and because
F_{y}
and
\rho
are positive (and hence self-adjoint). By cyclicity of the trace, this is equivalent to

{\rm tr}\big([F_{y},\rho]A\big)=0\qquad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q},\;\;\forall\;A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

(4.26)

By plugging in
A=E_{ij}
for all the values of
i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, this shows all components of the matrix
[F_{y},\rho]
must vanish, i.e.

[F_{y},\rho]=0\qquad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q}.

(4.27)

(
i
\Leftarrow
ii
)
Suppose that condition (
4.20
) holds. In what follows, we will prove that
G
as defined in (
4.23
) is a Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega)
.
The Bayes condition is satisfied by (
4.23
) because
q_{y}G(A)_{y}={\rm tr}(\rho AF_{y})
for all
y\in Y\setminus N_{q}
, and the Bayes condition holds automatically on
y\in N_{q}
by the first paragraph in the proof.
Furthermore,
G
is easily seen to be unital. What is left to prove is that
G
is in fact CP. Since the trace is CP, it suffices to focus on the formula when
y\in Y\setminus N_{q}
. Note that since both
\rho
and
F_{y}
are positive and commute by assumption
ii
, their product
\rho F_{y}=F_{y}\rho
is positive. Hence,

{\rm tr}(\rho_{y}AF_{y})={\rm tr}\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\rho F_{y}}}(A)\right)\qquad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q},\;\;\forall\;A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

(4.28)

This shows
G
is CP.

(
ii
\Leftrightarrow
iii
)
This follows immediately from the functional calculus, the fact that the
F_{y}
are positive operators, and since
[F_{y},\rho]=0
holds automatically for all
y\in N_{q}
.

(
iii
\Rightarrow
iv
)
Let
\varphi:=\sum_{y\in Y}\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{F_{y}}}
. Then
\varphi
is completely positive unital and trace-preserving. Indeed, unitality follows from the fact that
F
is a POVM. Furthermore, trace-preservation of
\varphi
follows from the fact that
\varphi^{*}=\varphi
and
\varphi
is unital if and only if
\varphi^{*}
is trace-preserving. By Kribs’ theorem (Theorem
4.14
),
\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)=\mathrm{Alg}(\varphi)^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Now, assume
\rho
satisfies condition
iii
. Then, by the functional calculus,
\rho\in\mathrm{Alg}(\varphi)^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Hence, by Kribs’ theorem,
\rho\in\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)
. In fact, combining earlier observations, we have shown

\sum_{y\in Y}\sqrt{F_{y}}\rho\sqrt{F_{y}}=\rho=\sum_{y\in Y\setminus N_{q}}\sqrt{F_{y}}\rho\sqrt{F_{y}}.

(4.29)

(
iv
\Rightarrow
iii
)
Assume
\rho\in\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)
so that
\rho
satisfies
iv
. Then by Kribs’ theorem again,
\rho\in\mathrm{Alg}(\varphi)^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Since the commutant
\mathrm{Alg}(\varphi)^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
equals the commutant
F\big({{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\big)^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
(by the functional calculus and properties of commutants),
\rho
satisfies
iii
.
∎

Remark 4.30
.

Proposition
4.19
generalizes our result for projection-valued measures (PVMs)
[
40
, Theorem 6.28]
. Indeed, if
F
defines a PVM (i.e. a unital
∗
-homomorphism, where each
F_{y}
is an orthogonal projection), then
[F_{y},\rho]=0
for all
y\in Y
holds if and only if
\rho=\sum_{y\in Y}F_{y}\rho F_{y},
i.e.
\rho
equals its Lüders projection with respect to the PVM.

Remark 4.31
.

Unitality, self-adjointness, and the Bayes condition were sufficient to imply that
G
(when evaluated on
Y\setminus N_{q}
) is CP in the proof of Proposition
4.19
.
This remark will be relevant later when we discuss our more general quantum Bayes’ theorem.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
such that
\omega\circ F=\xi
, one could ask when a Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega)
exists. This question could not be analyzed in the disintegration setting because there are no
∗
-homomorphisms from
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
into
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
when
n>1
. The reason
F
is called a collection of states is because for each
x\in X
, the functional
\mathrm{ev}_{x}\circ F
defines a state on
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
. Let
\sigma_{x}
be the density matrix associated to this state. Since
F
is state-preserving, this means that
\sigma=\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}\sigma_{x}
, where
p_{x}:=\omega(e_{x})
. This follows from

{\rm tr}(\sigma B)=\omega\big(F(B)\big)=\omega\left(\sum_{x\in X}F_{x}(B)e_{x}\right)=\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}{\rm tr}(\sigma_{x}B)\qquad\forall\;B\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(4.32)

Thus, we have a convex combination of states forming
\xi
. A Bayesian inverse in this situation is a CPU map
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\mathrel{\hbox to16.89pt{\vbox to11.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.44347pt\lower-6.99443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

, which itself is determined by the images of the basis vectors

.

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a density matrix with associated support denoted by
P
. There exists a unique positive (semidefinite) matrix
\hat{\sigma}
whose support equals
P
and such that
\sigma\hat{\sigma}=P=\hat{\sigma}\sigma
. When
P=\mathds{1}_{n}
, then
\hat{\sigma}=\sigma^{-1}
.

Proof sketch.

This is a simple linear algebra exercise: diagonalize
\sigma
, invert the non-zero entries, and then undiagonalize.
∎

Notation 4.34
.

The matrix
\hat{\sigma}
in Lemma
4.33
is called the
pseudoinverse
of
\sigma
.

Proposition 4.35
.

for the pair

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.03 -5.34 L 9.2 -5.34 C 8.17 -5.34 7.65 -6.52 6.62 -6.52 C 5.87 -6.52 5.22 -5.95 4.55 -5.34 C 3.88 -4.74 3.23 -4.16 2.48 -4.16 C 1.74 -4.16 1.09 -4.74 0.42 -5.34 C -0.26 -5.95 -0.9 -6.52 -1.65 -6.52 C -2.4 -6.52 -3.04 -5.95 -3.72 -5.34 C -4.39 -4.74 -5.04 -4.16 -5.78 -4.16 L -10.58 -5.34 L -10.58 -5.34}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.44936pt}{-3.85971pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathbb{C}}})
exists if and only if

p_{x}[\sigma,\sigma_{x}]=0\qquad\forall\;x\in X.

(4.36)

When this commuting condition holds,

G(e_{x})=p_{x}\left(\hat{\sigma}\sigma_{x}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}\right)\qquad\forall\;x\in X.

(4.37)

determines a Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega)
.

Proof.

In what follows, we set
G_{x}:=G(e_{x})
.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Assume a Bayesian inverse
G
exists.
Since
G
is positive and unital, this means
G_{x}\geq 0
for all
x\in X
and
\sum_{x\in X}G_{x}=\mathds{1}_{n},
respectively.
Plugging in
B:=E_{ji}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and
A:=e_{x}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
into the Bayes condition (
3.28
) gives

(\sigma G_{x})_{ij}={\rm tr}(\sigma G_{x}E_{ji})\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:quantumBayesequation})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\omega\big(e_{x}F(E_{ji})\big)=p_{x}\mathrm{ev}_{x}\big(F(E_{ji})\big)=p_{x}{\rm tr}(\sigma_{x}E_{ji})=p_{x}(\sigma_{x})_{ij},

(4.38)

i.e.

\sigma G_{x}=p_{x}\sigma_{x}\qquad\forall\;x\in X.

(4.39)

Since the right-hand-side of this is self-adjoint, the left-hand-side must be as well. This entails the condition

[G_{x},\sigma]=0\qquad\forall\;x\in X.

(4.40)

Thus,

p_{x}\sigma\sigma_{x}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:sigmaGxpx})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sigma\sigma G_{x}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Gxsigma})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sigma G_{x}\sigma\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:sigmaGxpx})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}p_{x}\sigma_{x}\sigma,

(4.41)

which proves (
4.36
).

(
\Leftarrow
)
Conversely, suppose this commuting condition holds.
Then
p_{x}[P_{\xi},\sigma_{x}]=0
by the functional calculus for
\sigma
. Hence,

p_{x}\sigma_{x}\hat{\sigma}=p_{x}P_{\xi}\sigma_{x}\hat{\sigma}=\hat{\sigma}(p_{x}\sigma\sigma_{x})\hat{\sigma}=\hat{\sigma}(p_{x}\sigma_{x}\sigma)\hat{\sigma}=p_{x}\hat{\sigma}\sigma_{x}P_{\xi}=p_{x}\hat{\sigma}\sigma_{x}.

(4.42)

Thus, the matrix
G_{x}
is positive because
the product of two commuting positive operators is positive and since projections are positive.
The map
G
is unital because

G(1_{X})=\sum_{x\in X}G_{x}=\hat{\sigma}\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}\sigma_{x}+\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}P_{\xi}^{\perp}=\hat{\sigma}\sigma+P_{\xi}^{\perp}=P_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}=\mathds{1}_{n}.

(4.43)

To see that
G
satisfies the Bayes condition,
first note that
P_{\mathrm{ev}_{x}\circ F}\leq P_{\xi}
because
[p_{x}\sigma_{x},\sigma]=0
and
p_{x}\sigma_{x}\leq\sigma
imply
[P_{\mathrm{ev}_{x}\circ F},P_{\xi}]=0
. Hence,

{\rm tr}\big(\sigma G(e_{x})B)=p_{x}{\rm tr}(P_{\xi}\sigma_{x}B)=p_{x}{\rm tr}(\sigma_{x}B)=\omega\big(e_{x}F(B)\big).

(4.44)

Since
x\in X
and
B\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
were arbitrary, this proves the claim.
∎

4.4
After wave collapse

Set
{{\mathcal{A}}}:=\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
, let
H\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
be self-adjoint with spectrum
\sigma(H),
and let
\{P_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\sigma(H)}
be the orthogonal projections onto the corresponding eigenspaces of
H
. Set
F:=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(H)}\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\lambda}}
. Let
\rho
be a density matrix on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
with corresponding state
\omega:={\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)
. Let
\sigma=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(H)}P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}
be the density matrix associated to
\omega\circ F
. Set
\xi:={\rm tr}(\sigma\;\cdot\;)
. In this section, we will analyze the conditions for
(F,\omega)
to have a Bayesian inverse. This setup describes what happens to a system
{{\mathcal{A}}}
after the observable
H
has been measured and the result of the measurement has been forgotten or is hidden (cf. Lecture 11 of
[
51
]
). One can also think of
\sigma
as describing the statistical ensemble of density matrices that the original density matrix
\rho
“collapses” into upon measurement of the observable
H
. The corresponding non-negative numbers
{\rm tr}(\rho P_{\lambda})
are the probabilities that the eigenvalue
\lambda
is obtained after the measurement. In a single run of such an experiment, the density matrix
\rho
reduces to
P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}
.

Proposition 4.45
.

Using the notation in the previous paragraph,
(F,\omega)
has a Bayesian inverse
G
if and only if

\rho=\sigma
. When a Bayesian inverse exists, it is unique and is given by
G=F^{*}=F=\sum_{\lambda}\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\lambda}}
.

The proof of this proposition will be postponed until after our main theorem (Theorem
5.62
), more precisely after Lemma
5.101
and Definition
5.108
.

5
A quantum Bayes’ theorem for matrix algebras

In this section, we will find conditions for the existence and uniqueness of Bayesian inference on matrix algebras.

P_{\xi}G(A)=\hat{\sigma}F^{*}(\rho A)\qquad\forall\;A\in{{\mathcal{A}}},

(5.2)

or equivalently

G^{*}(BP_{\xi})=F(B\hat{\sigma})\rho\qquad\forall\;B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

(5.3)

Conversely, any linear map
G

5.2
) is a Bayes map.
In particular, if
\sigma
is invertible, then

G(A)=\sigma^{-1}F^{*}(\rho A)\qquad\forall\;A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}

(5.4)

is the unique linear (necessarily unital) Bayes map.

Proof.

Suppose
G

(F,\omega)
. Plugging in
B:=E_{ji}
into (

) gives

\big(\sigma G(A)\big)_{ij}={\rm tr}\big(\sigma G(A)E_{ji}\big)\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:quantumBayesequation})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}{\rm tr}\big(\rho AF(E_{ji})\big)\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:HSdualcondition})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}{\rm tr}\big(F^{*}(\rho A)E_{ji}\big)=\big(F^{*}(\rho A)\big)_{ij}

(5.5)

for all
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Since this equation is true for all
i
and
j
, it holds as matrix equations, i.e.
\sigma G(A)=F^{*}(\rho A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
.
Multiplying both sides by
\hat{\sigma}
on the left gives (
5.2
).

5.3
) is equivalent to (
5.2
).
This argument is similar to earlier ones involving the trace (see the proof of Lemma
3.42
for example).

), we have

)

.

(5.7)

which is the Bayes condition.

Finally, the formula (
5.4
) and uniqueness of
G
when
\sigma
is invertible follows from (
5.2
) and Lemma
3.20
.
∎

Proposition
5.1
tells us that when
\sigma
is invertible, a unique unital Bayes map exists, but we have made no such claim that this map is positive. Without requiring positivity, we already have a solution to the existence of unital Bayes maps.

.

uniquely determined by the projections

P_{\xi}G(A):=\hat{\sigma}F^{*}(\rho A),\quad P_{\xi}^{\perp}G(A)P_{\xi}:=P_{\xi}^{\perp}F^{*}(A\rho)\hat{\sigma},\quad\text{ and }\quad P_{\xi}^{\perp}G(A)P_{\xi}^{\perp}:=\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(A)P_{\xi}^{\perp}

(5.9)

for all
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}

is a unital Bayes map for
(F,\omega)
.

Proof.

First note that the projections specify
G
everywhere since

G(A)=P_{\xi}G(A)+P_{\xi}^{\perp}G(A)P_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}G(A)P_{\xi}^{\perp}
.
The map
G
satisfies condition (
5.2
) and therefore satisfies the Bayes condition by Proposition
5.1
. The calculation

G(\mathds{1}_{m})=\hat{\sigma}F^{*}(\rho)+P_{\xi}^{\perp}F^{*}(\rho)\hat{\sigma}+\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(\mathds{1}_{m})P_{\xi}^{\perp}=\hat{\sigma}\sigma+P_{\xi}^{\perp}\sigma\hat{\sigma}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}=P_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}=\mathds{1}_{n}

(5.10)

proves that
G
is unital.
∎

Therefore, causal quantum Bayes maps exist, but they might not be implementable as quantum operations. In what follows, we will find conditions on
F
and
\rho
that guarantee when a (CPU) Bayesian inverse exists.

be a state with associated support
P_{\xi}
. The subalgebra
P_{\xi}{{\mathcal{B}}}P_{\xi}
will be called the
supported corner
of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
with respect to the state
\xi
.

When the density matrix
\sigma
is invertible, a necessary condition for a Bayes map to be completely positive is that it preserve the involution (this is the implication
i
\Rightarrow
vi
in Proposition
5.12
below). This result is closely related to a theorem of Nakamura, Takesaki, and Umegaki, which states that a positive conditional expectation is automatically completely positive
[
36
]
.
In our case, we are working with morphisms that are a bit weaker than conditional expectations (the latter of which can be viewed as assuming
F
to be an injective
∗
-homomorphism) and we will only assume that (the supported corner of) our Bayes maps are
*
-preserving rather than positive.
11
11

11

We would like to thank Luca Giorgetti and Alessio Ranallo for discussions clarifying some of these points. Further details elaborating these, and other, points will appear elsewhere.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
F,G,\omega,\rho,\xi
, and
\sigma
be as in Proposition
5.1

with
G
a Bayes map for
(F,\omega)
. Then the following conditions are equivalent.

i.

\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is
*
-preserving.

ii.

P_{\xi}F^{*}(\rho A)\sigma=\sigma F^{*}(A\rho)P_{\xi}
for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
.

iii.

F(\sigma B)\rho=\rho F(B\sigma)
for all
B\in P_{\xi}\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})P_{\xi}
.

iv.

Let
U\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a unitary matrix diagonalizing
\rho
, i.e.
U\rho U^{{\dagger}}=\boldsymbol{\rho}
, with
\boldsymbol{\rho}
diagonal. Let
{}_{U}F:=\mathrm{Ad}_{U}\circ F
.
Then, the Choi matrix of

F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}:=F^{*}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}

satisfies

(\boldsymbol{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma})\mathrm{Choi}\big(F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big)(\mathds{1}_{m}\otimes P_{\xi})=(\mathds{1}_{m}\otimes P_{\xi})\mathrm{Choi}\big(F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big)(\boldsymbol{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma})

(5.13)

(hence,
\mathrm{Choi}\big(F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big)
commutes with
\boldsymbol{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\rho}\otimes\sigma^{-1}
when
P_{\xi}=\mathds{1}_{n}
).

v.

Let
U\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a unitary matrix diagonalizing
\rho
as in item
iv
and let
V\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a unitary matrix simultaneously diagonalizing
\sigma,\hat{\sigma},
and
P_{\xi}
, i.e.
V^{{\dagger}}\sigma V=\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
V^{{\dagger}}\hat{\sigma}V=\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, and
V^{{\dagger}}P_{\xi}V=\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}
, with bold symbols representing diagonal matrices and states.
12
12

12

This can be done because
\sigma,\hat{\sigma},
and
P_{\xi}
are self-adjoint and commute with each other and are therefore simultaneously diagonalizable via the same unitary.

Let
{}_{U}F_{V}:=\mathrm{Ad}_{U}\circ F\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{V}
and
{}_{V^{{\dagger}}}G_{U^{{\dagger}}}:=\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{{\dagger}}}\circ G\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}
. Then,
the Choi matrix of
{}_{U}F_{V}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}

satisfies

\big[\mathrm{Choi}\big({}_{U}F_{V}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\big),\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\rho}\big]=0.

(5.14)

vi.

\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is completely positive.

Proof.

you found me!

(
i
\Rightarrow
ii
) Suppose that
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is
*
-preserving. Then

\hat{\sigma}F^{*}(\rho A^{{\dagger}})P_{\xi}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qBayesianinversepartialsupport})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}P_{\xi}G\big(A^{{\dagger}}\big)P_{\xi}\overset{\text{\ref{item:sa1}}}{=\joinrel=}\big(P_{\xi}G(A)P_{\xi}\big)^{{\dagger}}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qBayesianinversepartialsupport})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\big(\hat{\sigma}F^{*}(\rho A)P_{\xi}\big)^{{\dagger}}=P_{\xi}F^{*}(A^{{\dagger}}\rho)\hat{\sigma},

(5.15)

where we have also used that
F^{*}
is
*
-preserving because
F^{*}
is CP. Multiplying the left and right by
\sigma
and using the properties of
\sigma,P_{\xi},
and
\hat{\sigma}
gives the claim.

(
ii
\Rightarrow
iii
)
Since
P_{\xi}F^{*}(\rho A)\sigma=\sigma F^{*}(A\rho)P_{\xi}
is a matrix equation, the corresponding
ij
components are also equal. By using the properties of the trace,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 141.40869pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.6451pt\raise 57.59912pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.91946pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big(\sigma F^{*}(A\rho)P_{\xi}\big)_{ij}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 46.9625pt\raise 19.1997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm tr}\big(\sigma F^{*}(A\rho)P_{\xi}E_{ji}\big)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern tr}\big(F(\sigma P_{\xi}E_{ji}P_{\xi})\rho A\big)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-141.40869pt\raise-19.1997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm tr}\big(\sigma P_{\xi}E_{ji}P_{\xi}F^{*}(\rho A)\big)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-135.43472pt\raise 19.1997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm tr}\big(P_{\xi}F^{*}(\rho A)\sigma E_{ji}\big)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-84.35344pt\raise A)\sigma\big)_{ij}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.37502pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma=P_{\xi}\sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-126.80655pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.37502pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma=\sigma P_{\xi}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-111.56682pt\raise 44.02399pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\text{Lem~\ref{lem:matrixunitsandtrace}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.8889pt\raise 68.59912pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\text{\ref{item:sa2}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}.

(5.16)

The bottom of this gives

{\rm tr}\Big(\big(F(\sigma P_{\xi}E_{ji}P_{\xi})\rho-\rho F(P_{\xi}E_{ji}P_{\xi}\sigma)\big)A\Big)=0\qquad\forall\;A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(5.17)

For this to be true, the term to the left of
A
must vanish. In other words,

F(\sigma P_{\xi}E_{ji}P_{\xi})\rho=\rho F(P_{\xi}E_{ji}P_{\xi}\sigma)

(5.18)

for all
i,j
. Hence,
iii
holds.

(
iii
\Rightarrow
i
)
Since
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is
*
-preserving if and only if
G^{*}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}
is
*
-preserving by Lemma
4.16
, it suffices to prove the latter. In this case,

\begin{split}G^{*}(P_{\xi}B^{{\dagger}}P_{\xi})&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qBayesianinversepartialsupportdual})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}F(P_{\xi}B^{{\dagger}}\hat{\sigma})\rho=\big(\rho F(\hat{\sigma}BP_{\xi})\big)^{{\dagger}}\overset{\text{Lem~\ref{lem:inverseuptosupport}}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\big(\rho F(\hat{\sigma}B\hat{\sigma}\sigma)\big)^{{\dagger}}\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{item:sa3}}}{=\joinrel=}\big(F(\sigma\hat{\sigma}B\hat{\sigma})\rho\big)^{{\dagger}}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qBayesianinversepartialsupportdual})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}G^{*}(\sigma\hat{\sigma}BP_{\xi})^{{\dagger}}=G^{*}(P_{\xi}BP_{\xi})^{{\dagger}}.\end{split}

(5.19)

(
i
\Leftrightarrow
iv
)
Recall that
G
is a Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega)
if and only if
G_{U^{{\dagger}}}:=G\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{\dagger}}
is a Bayesian inverse of
({}_{U}F,\boldsymbol{\omega}:=\omega\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{\dagger}})
(cf. Lemma
3.39
). Let
\{p_{i}\}
denote the eigenvalues of
\rho
.
Recall that a linear map
\varphi
between matrix algebras is
*
-preserving if and only if its Choi matrix is self-adjoint,
\mathrm{Choi}(\varphi)^{{\dagger}}=\mathrm{Choi}(\varphi)
.
In this case,
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is
*
-preserving if and only if
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G_{U^{{\dagger}}}
is
*
-preserving if and only if
\mathrm{Choi}(\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G_{U^{{\dagger}}})
is self-adjoint, and the latter is given by

\begin{split}\mathrm{Choi}\big(\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big)&=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes P_{\xi}G_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)P_{\xi}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qBayesianinversepartialsupport})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\hat{\sigma}F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big(\boldsymbol{\rho}E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)P_{\xi}\\
&=\sum_{i,j}p_{i}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\hat{\sigma}F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)P_{\xi}=\sum_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\rho}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\hat{\sigma}F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)P_{\xi}\\
&=(\boldsymbol{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma})\mathrm{Choi}\big(F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\big)(\mathds{1}_{m}\otimes P_{\xi}).\end{split}

(5.20)

Taking the adjoint of this and equating it to itself proves the claim.

(
i
\Leftrightarrow
v
)
Set

\hat{q}_{j}:=\begin{cases}q_{j}^{-1}&\mbox{ when $q_{j}>0$}\\
0&\mbox{ when $q_{j}=0$}\end{cases}\;\;,

(5.21)

where
\{q_{j}\}
consists of the eigenvalues of
\sigma
.
The Choi matrix of
{}_{U}G^{*}_{V}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}
is equal to

\begin{split}\mathrm{Choi}&\big({}_{U}G^{*}_{V}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\big)=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(n)}\otimes UG^{*}\big(V\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}E_{ij}^{(n)}\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}V^{{\dagger}}\big)U^{{\dagger}}\\
&=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(n)}\otimes UG^{*}\big(P_{\xi}VE_{ij}^{(n)}V^{{\dagger}}P_{\xi}\big)U^{{\dagger}}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qBayesianinversepartialsupportdual})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(n)}\otimes UF\big(P_{\xi}VE_{ij}^{(n)}V^{{\dagger}}\hat{\sigma}\big)\rho U^{{\dagger}}\\
&=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(n)}\otimes UF\big(V\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}\!\!\underbrace{E_{ij}^{(n)}\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{E_{ij}^{(n)}\hat{q}_{j}\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\!\!V^{{\dagger}}\big)U^{{\dagger}}U\rho U^{{\dagger}}=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(n)}\hat{q}_{j}\otimes UF\big(V\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}E_{ij}^{(n)}\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}\big)U^{{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\\
&=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(n)}\hat{\sigma}\otimes{}_{U}F_{V}\!\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\big(E_{ij}^{(n)}\big)\right)\boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathrm{Choi}\big({}_{U}F_{V}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\big)(\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\rho}).\end{split}

(5.22)

This is self-adjoint if and only if
G^{*}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}
is
*
-preserving, which holds if and only if
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is
*
-preserving.

(
i
\Rightarrow
vi
)
In this part of the proof, we will assume that
\rho
and
\sigma
have been diagonalized with all non-vanishing eigenvalues appearing on the top-left block. The reason we can do this is the following. As in Lemma
3.39
, let
U
be a unitary such that
U^{{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\rho}U=\rho
with
\boldsymbol{\rho}
diagonal, and let
V
be a unitary such that
V^{{\dagger}}\sigma V=\boldsymbol{\sigma},V^{{\dagger}}P_{\xi}V=\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}},
and
V^{{\dagger}}\hat{\sigma}V=\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, where the bold matrices are diagonal. By Lemma
3.39
,
G
is a Bayesian inverse of
(F,\omega)
if and only if
{}_{V^{{\dagger}}}G_{U^{{\dagger}}}
is a Bayesian inverse of
({}_{U}F_{V},\boldsymbol{\omega}:=\omega\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}})
. Since
U
and
V
are unitary,
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is CP (or
*
-preserving) if and only if
\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{{\dagger}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}
is CP (or
*
-preserving). Since the latter map equals

\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{{\dagger}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}=\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{{\dagger}}}\circ G\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{{\dagger}}}=\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\circ{}_{V^{{\dagger}}}G_{U^{{\dagger}}},

(5.23)

the map
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is CP (or
*
-preserving) if and only if
\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\circ{}_{V^{{\dagger}}}G_{U^{{\dagger}}}
is. This has established that we can assume
\rho
and
\sigma
are diagonal. As such,
let
r
be the rank of
\sigma
.
Write
F=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{\alpha}}
, where
V_{\alpha}
is an
m\times n
matrix whose
ij
-th entry will be written as
V^{\alpha}_{ij}
for the purposes of this proof (and later proofs).
Then the Choi matrix of
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is given by

\begin{split}\mathrm{Choi}&\big(\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G\big)=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes P_{\xi}G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)P_{\xi}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qBayesianinversepartialsupport})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\hat{\sigma}F^{*}\big(\rho E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)P_{\xi}\\
&=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\hat{\sigma}F^{*}\big(p_{i}E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)P_{\xi}=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\left(p_{i}\hat{\sigma}\sum_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}E_{ij}^{(m)}V_{\alpha}P_{\xi}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\left(\begin{bmatrix}p_{i}/q_{1}&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&p_{i}/q_{r}&0\\
0&\cdots&0&0_{n-r}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{jn}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\overline{V^{\alpha}_{in}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\overline{V^{\alpha}_{in}}V^{\alpha}_{jn}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathds{1}_{r}&0\\
0&0_{n-r}\end{bmatrix}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{1}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\frac{p_{i}}{q_{1}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{jr}&0\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{p_{i}}{q_{r}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ir}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\frac{p_{i}}{q_{r}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ir}}V^{\alpha}_{jr}&0\\
0&\cdots&0&0_{n-r}\\
\end{bmatrix},\end{split}

(5.24)

while taking the adjoint of the Choi matrix gives

\mathrm{Choi}\big(\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G\big)^{{\dagger}}=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}\frac{p_{j}}{q_{1}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\frac{p_{j}}{q_{r}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{jr}&0\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{p_{j}}{q_{1}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ir}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\frac{p_{j}}{q_{r}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ir}}V^{\alpha}_{jr}&0\\
0&\cdots&0&0_{n-r}\\
\end{bmatrix}

(5.25)

due to the summation over all
i,j
.
Self-adjointness of the Choi matrix
equates (
5.24
) with (
5.25
) and gives

\frac{p_{i}}{q_{k}}\sum_{\alpha}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ik}}V^{\alpha}_{jl}=\frac{p_{j}}{q_{l}}\sum_{\alpha}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ik}}V^{\alpha}_{jl}\qquad\forall\;k,l\in\{1,\dots,r\},\;\;i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}.

(5.26)

This condition is so strong that it actually implies positivity of this matrix. Indeed, if we set

\mathfrak{V}_{\alpha}:=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{p_{1}}{q_{1}}}V^{\alpha}_{11}&\cdots&\!\!\sqrt{\frac{p_{1}}{q_{r}}}V^{\alpha}_{1r}&0&\cdots&0&{\small\bullet\;\bullet\;\bullet}&\sqrt{\frac{p_{m}}{q_{1}}}V^{\alpha}_{m1}&\cdots&\!\!\sqrt{\frac{p_{m}}{q_{r}}}V^{\alpha}_{mr}&0&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix},

(5.27)

then

\sum_{\alpha}\mathfrak{V}_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}\mathfrak{V}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}^{(m)}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{p_{i}p_{j}}{q_{1}^{2}}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\sqrt{\frac{p_{i}p_{j}}{q_{1}q_{r}}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{i1}}V^{\alpha}_{jr}&0\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots\\
\sqrt{\frac{p_{i}p_{j}}{q_{r}q_{1}}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ir}}V^{\alpha}_{j1}&\cdots&\sqrt{\frac{p_{i}p_{j}}{q_{r}^{2}}}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ir}}V^{\alpha}_{jr}&0\\
0&\cdots&0&0_{n-r}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\mathrm{Choi}\big(\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G\big).

(5.28)

To see the last equality in (
5.28
), first notice that condition (
5.26
) can be rewritten as

p_{i}q_{l}\beta_{ijkl}=p_{j}q_{k}\beta_{ijkl},\quad\text{where}\quad\beta_{ijkl}:=\sum_{\alpha}\overline{V^{\alpha}_{ik}}V^{\alpha}_{jl}.

(5.29)

If
\beta_{ijkl}=0
, then
\sqrt{p_{i}q_{l}}\beta_{ijkl}=\sqrt{p_{j}q_{k}}\beta_{ijkl}
.
If
\beta_{ijkl}\neq 0
, then
p_{i}q_{l}=p_{j}q_{k}
so again
\sqrt{p_{i}q_{l}}\beta_{ijkl}=\sqrt{p_{j}q_{k}}\beta_{ijkl}
. Thus, we have

\sqrt{\frac{p_{i}}{q_{k}}}\beta_{ijkl}=\sqrt{\frac{p_{j}}{q_{l}}}\beta_{ijkl}\qquad\forall\;k,l\in\{1,\dots,r\},\;i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}.

(5.30)

Hence,

\sqrt{\frac{p_{i}p_{j}}{q_{k}q_{l}}}\beta_{ijkl}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:betaijkl})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{k}}\beta_{ijkl},

(5.31)

which proves (
5.28
).
This shows that
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
is completely positive by Choi’s theorem.

(
vi
\Rightarrow
i
) This direction is immediate since all positive matrices are self-adjoint.
∎

Corollary 5.32
.

Let
F,G,\omega,\rho,\xi
, and
\sigma
be as in Proposition
5.1
with
G
a Bayes map for
(F,\omega)
. Let
F=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{\alpha}}
be a Kraus decomposition of
F
. Suppose further that any of the equivalent conditions in Proposition
5.12
hold. Then
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
has a Kraus decomposition given by

\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}}}\circ F^{*}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\rho}}=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}}V_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}\sqrt{\rho}}.

(5.33)

Proof.

A Choi matrix for
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
was constructed in (
5.24
) in the proof of Proposition
5.12
. It was shown that
\mathrm{Choi}(\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G)=\sum_{\alpha}\mathfrak{V}_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}\mathfrak{V}_{\alpha}
. Hence, if we demand
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G
has a Kraus decomposition of the form
\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G=\sum_{\beta}\mathrm{Ad}_{W_{\beta}}
, then it must be the case that

(\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G)(E_{ij})=\sum_{\beta}W_{\beta}E_{ij}W_{\beta}^{{\dagger}}=\sum_{\beta}W_{\beta}e_{i}e_{j}^{{\dagger}}W_{\beta}^{{\dagger}}=\sum_{\beta}(W_{\beta}e_{i})(W_{\beta}e_{j})^{{\dagger}}

(5.34)

But this is precisely the
ij
-th block of the Choi matrix. Hence, we can set

W_{\alpha}:=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{{p}_{1}}{q_{1}}}\overline{V_{11}^{\alpha}}&\cdots&\sqrt{\frac{{p}_{m}}{q_{1}}}\overline{V_{m1}^{\alpha}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\sqrt{\frac{{p}_{1}}{q_{r}}}\overline{V_{1r}^{\alpha}}&\cdots&\sqrt{\frac{{p}_{m}}{q_{r}}}\overline{V_{mr}^{\alpha}}\\
0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0\\
\end{bmatrix}=\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}}V_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}\sqrt{\rho}.

(5.35)

Note that the expression on the right still holds even if we did not choose a basis in which
\sigma
and
\rho
have been diagonalized. To see this, we temporarily use the notation from the proof of (
i
\Rightarrow
vi
) in Proposition
5.12
. In doing so, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ G&=\mathrm{Ad}_{V}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\boldsymbol{P_{\xi}}}\circ{}_{V^{{\dagger}}}G_{U^{{\dagger}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U}=\mathrm{Ad}_{V}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}\circ{}_{V^{{\dagger}}}F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U}\\
&=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{V}\circ{}_{V^{{\dagger}}}F^{*}_{U^{{\dagger}}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{U}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\rho}}=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}}V_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}\sqrt{\rho}}\end{split}

(5.36)

by the way in which the bold versions of these mathematical objects have been defined.
∎

.

{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.8 -6.56 L 9.97 -6.56 C 8.94 -6.56 8.42 -7.75 7.39 -7.75 C 6.64 -7.75 5.99 -7.17 5.32 -6.56 C 4.64 -5.96 4 -5.38 3.25 -5.38 C 2.5 -5.38 1.86 -5.96 1.18 -6.56 C 0.51 -7.17 -0.13 -7.75 -0.88 -7.75 C -1.63 -7.75 -2.28 -7.17 -2.95 -6.56 C -3.62 -5.96 -4.27 -5.38 -5.02 -5.38 L -11.35 -6.56 L -11.35 -6.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.00446pt}{-4.74443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
a CP map with Kraus operator decomposition
F=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{\alpha}}
. Set
\sigma:=F^{*}(\rho)
. The map

L:=\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{Ad}_{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}}V_{\alpha}^{{\dagger}}\sqrt{\rho}}
is what Leifer calls the Bayesian inverse of
F

[
33
,
34
]
, which he obtains by a variant of the Choi–Jamiolkowski isomorphism. This map was later described diagrammatically
by Coecke and Spekkens
[
10
]
and Jacobs
[
24
]

and was further enhanced in the work of Leifer and Spekkens
[
32
]
. There are several important comments to be made with regards to Leifer’s map versus ours.
First, the notion of a.e. equivalence was not discussed in any of these works and therefore the uniqueness of Bayesian inverses was not addressed. In particular, how should the Bayesian inverse be defined off the support (what we call the supported corner)?
Second, the map
H
is not in general unital unless
\sigma
is invertible. Furthermore, even if
\sigma
is invertible, the Bayesian inverse of the Bayesian inverse is
not
in general a.e. equivalent to the original map
F
unless
\rho
is
also
invertible. However, this restricts the analysis of CPU Bayesian inversion to states that have full support and therefore ignores, in particular, pure states. Third, our arrival at Corollary
5.32
comes at a cost. While Leifer makes no assumptions about the relationship between the density matrices and the map
F
to construct his Bayesian inverse
L
, we have enforced additional (in general non-trivial) constraints as in Proposition
5.12
to accomplish this task. This is due to our reliance on the categorical expression of the classical Bayes’ theorem presented in Theorem
2.23
, which was used to define Bayesian inversion in the non-commutative setting in Definition
3.26
. We make no claims as to which perspective is correct or wrong, and we suspect both have their appropriate domains of applicability.
One interesting curiosity is the arrival at the form of the Bayesian inverse in Corollary
5.32
through a more structural perspective.
Another benefit of our categorical approach is that it offers an explicit construction of the Bayesian inverse off of the support. Therefore, our definition is capable of handling density matrices regardless of their support. This will be explained in Theorem
5.62
after we go through a few more examples and preparation.

Proposition 5.38
.

A Bayesian inverse always exists for any pair of CPU maps of the form

(5.39)

where
V^{{\dagger}}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
is an isometry. A representative for such Bayesian inverse is given by

\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\ni A\xmapsto{G}V^{{\dagger}}AV+\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(A)(V^{{\dagger}}V)^{\perp}.

(5.40)

Before proving this, some explanation is needed. Note that since
V^{{\dagger}}
is an isometry, the
n\times n
matrix
V^{{\dagger}}V
is a projection. An interesting point about this result is that a Bayesian inverse of a Kraus rank one CPU map need not be Kraus rank one. We first prove a little lemma that will be used elsewhere as well.

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state. Set
\sigma:=V^{{\dagger}}\rho V
and write the corresponding state on
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
as
\xi
, so that
\xi=\omega\circ F
.
Then
V^{{\dagger}}V
is a projection satisfying

P_{\xi}\leq V^{{\dagger}}V\quad\text{ and }\quad(V^{{\dagger}}V)^{\perp}\leq P_{\xi}^{\perp}.

(5.42)

In particular,

P_{\xi}=P_{\xi}V^{{\dagger}}V=V^{{\dagger}}VP_{\xi}\quad\text{ and }\quad(V^{{\dagger}}V)^{\perp}=P_{\xi}^{\perp}(V^{{\dagger}}V)^{\perp}=(V^{{\dagger}}V)^{\perp}P_{\xi}^{\perp}.

(5.43)

Proof of Lemma
5.41
.

The fact that
V^{{\dagger}}V
is a projection follows from
V^{{\dagger}}
being an isometry.
Since

V^{{\dagger}}V\sigma=V^{{\dagger}}VV^{{\dagger}}\rho V=V^{{\dagger}}\rho V=\sigma=\sigma^{{\dagger}}=\big(V^{{\dagger}}V\sigma\big)^{{\dagger}}=\sigma^{{\dagger}}\big(V^{{\dagger}}V\big)^{{\dagger}}=\sigma V^{{\dagger}}V,

(5.44)

V^{{\dagger}}V
is a projection satisfying
\sigma V^{{\dagger}}V=\sigma=V^{{\dagger}}V\sigma
. Because
P_{\xi}
is the
smallest
projection satisfying this condition,
P_{\xi}\leq V^{{\dagger}}V
. The other claims follow from the properties of projections and orthogonal complements.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.38
.

The proof involves several small steps which will be done one at a time. These include showing that
G
is unital, completely positive, and finally that it satisfies the Bayes condition. The first two are simple to prove. Indeed,

G(\mathds{1}_{m})=V^{{\dagger}}V+\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(\mathds{1}_{m})(V^{{\dagger}}V)^{\perp}=V^{{\dagger}}V+(V^{{\dagger}}V)^{\perp}=\mathds{1}_{n}

(5.45)

proves unitality. Complete positivity can be proved in several ways. The first term is clearly completely positive while the latter term is the composite

\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\mathrel{\hbox to42.86pt{\vbox to13.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.42828pt\lower-8.10277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
