}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.85 -6.56 L 10.02 -6.56 C 8.99 -6.56 8.47 -7.75 7.44 -7.75 C 6.69 -7.75 6.05 -7.17 5.37 -6.56 C 4.7 -5.96 4.05 -5.38 3.31 -5.38 C 2.56 -5.38 1.91 -5.96 1.24 -6.56 C 0.56 -7.17 -0.08 -7.75 -0.83 -7.75 C -1.58 -7.75 -2.22 -7.17 -2.9 -6.56 C -3.57 -5.96 -4.21 -5.38 -4.96 -5.38 L -11.41 -6.56 L -11.41 -6.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.04347pt}{-4.74443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathcal{B}}}
is a Bayes map for
(F,\omega)
and
let
(x,y)\in X\times(Y\setminus N_{q})
.
Then
\mathrm{Ad}_{Q_{y}}\circ G_{yx}:\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n_{y}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is
*
-preserving if and only if
it is CP.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.15
,
\mathrm{Ad}_{Q_{y}}\circ G_{yx}
takes the form

(\mathrm{Ad}_{Q_{y}}\circ G_{yx})(A_{x})=\frac{p_{x}}{q_{y}}\widehat{\sigma_{y}}F^{*}_{xy}(\rho_{x}A_{x})Q_{y}\qquad\forall\;A_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(6.20)

If
p_{x}=0,
then
\mathrm{Ad}_{Q_{y}}\circ G_{yx}
is the zero map and is therefore CP. If
p_{x}\neq 0
, by Proposition
5.12
, all of the items listed in that proposition are equivalent to
\mathrm{Ad}_{Q_{y}}\circ G_{yx}
being
*
-preserving. In particular,
\mathrm{Ad}_{Q_{y}}\circ G_{yx}
is CP.
∎

Lemma 6.21
.

The following conditions on a linear map
{{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrel{\hbox to16.89pt{\vbox to11.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.44347pt\lower-6.99443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.8907pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathcal{B}}}
are equivalent.

i.

G
is unital.

ii.

G
satisfies

\sum_{x\in X}G_{yx}(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}})=\mathds{1}_{n_{y}}

for all
y\in Y
.

iii.

G
satisfies

\sum_{x\in X}{\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}\big(\mathrm{Choi}(G_{yx})\big)=\mathds{1}_{n_{y}}

for all
y\in Y
.

Theorem 6.22

(A Bayes’ theorem for finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras)
.

For each
x\in X
and
y\in Y\setminus N_{q}
, set (cf. Notation
6.1
)

\mathfrak{A}_{yx}:=\frac{p_{x}}{q_{y}}\!\sum_{i,j=1}^{m_{x}}E_{ij}^{(m_{x})}\!\otimes\widehat{\sigma_{y}}F_{xy}^{*}\big(\rho_{x}E_{ij}^{(m_{x})}\big)Q_{y}\;\text{ and }\;\mathfrak{B}_{yx}:=\frac{p_{x}}{q_{y}}\!\sum_{i,j=1}^{m_{x}}E_{ij}^{(m_{x})}\!\otimes\widehat{\sigma_{y}}F_{xy}^{*}\big(\rho_{x}E_{ij}^{(m_{x})}\big)Q_{y}^{\perp},

(6.23)

which are matrices in
\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{n_{y}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
.
Then
(F,\omega)
has a (CPU) Bayesian inverse if and only if

\mathfrak{A}_{yx}^{{\dagger}}=\mathfrak{A}_{yx}\qquad\forall\;(x,y)\in X\times(Y\setminus N_{q})

(6.24)

and

\sum_{x\in X}{\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}\left(\mathfrak{B}_{yx}^{{\dagger}}\widehat{\mathfrak{A}_{yx}}\mathfrak{B}_{yx}\right)\leq Q_{y}^{\perp}\qquad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q}.

(6.25)

The proof will be similar to the proof of Theorem
5.62
with a few minor changes. We will therefore spell this out, but on occasion we will shorten arguments which would otherwise be repetitive.

Proof of Theorem
6.22
.

you found me!

(
\Rightarrow
)
Suppose that
(F,\omega)
has a Bayesian inverse
G
. Then by Proposition
6.15
,

(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}}\otimes Q_{y})\big(\mathrm{Choi}(G_{yx})\big)(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}}\otimes Q_{y})=\mathfrak{A}_{yx}\quad\text{ and }\quad(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}}\otimes Q_{y})\big(\mathrm{Choi}(G_{yx})\big)(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}}\otimes Q_{y}^{\perp})=\mathfrak{B}_{yx}

(6.26)

hold for all
(x,y)\in X\times(Y\setminus N_{q})
.
The rest of the proof in this direction follows similar arguments as in the proof of Theorem
5.62
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Conversely, suppose the two conditions hold. For
y\in N_{q}
, set

\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\ni A_{x}\mapsto G_{yx}(A_{x}):=\frac{{\rm tr}(A_{x})}{m_{x}|X|}\mathds{1}_{n_{y}}\qquad\forall\;x\in X.

(6.27)

Note that
G_{yx}
is completely positive and satisfies

\sum_{x\in X}G_{yx}(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}})=\mathds{1}_{n_{y}}\qquad\forall\;y\in N_{q}.

(6.28)

For
y\in Y\setminus N_{q}
, most of the proof follows through as in the proof of Theorem
5.62
. In particular, one builds
G_{yx}
in terms of its Choi matrix by using (
6.26
).
The first main difference occurs in defining
\mathfrak{C}_{yx}\equiv(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}}\otimes Q_{y}^{\perp})\big(\mathrm{Choi}(G_{yx})\big)(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}}\otimes Q_{y}^{\perp})
, which can be set as

\mathfrak{C}_{yx}:=\mathfrak{B}_{yx}^{{\dagger}}\widehat{\mathfrak{A}_{yx}}\mathfrak{B}_{yx}+\frac{1}{m_{x}|X|}\left(\mathds{1}_{m_{x}}\otimes\left(Q_{y}^{\perp}-\sum_{x^{\prime}\in X}{\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{m_{x^{\prime}}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}\Big(\mathfrak{B}_{yx^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\widehat{\mathfrak{A}_{yx^{\prime}}}\mathfrak{B}_{yx^{\prime}}\Big)\right)\right).

(6.29)

Note that
\mathfrak{C}_{yx}\geq 0
and satisfies

\sum_{x\in X}{\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}(\mathfrak{C}_{yx})=Q_{y}^{\perp}.

(6.30)

With these definitions,
G
has been defined everywhere and automatically satisfies the Bayes condition by the assumptions made. Finally, (
6.28
), (
6.30
),

\sum_{x\in X}{\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}(\mathfrak{A}_{yx})=Q_{y},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sum_{x\in X}{\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{m_{x}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}(\mathfrak{B}_{yx})=0\qquad\forall\;y\in Y\setminus N_{q}

(6.31)

show
G
is unital by Lemma
6.21
.
∎

In the following example, we will describe when Bayesian inverses exist for quantum instruments.

of CPU maps (note the difference and that
F_{y}
was not required to be unital). At present, we have not yet found a simpler description of when Bayesian inverses exist other than to demand the assumptions in Theorem
6.22
. Nevertheless, if one has an explicit instrument with known states, then it should not be too difficult to apply the criteria of Theorem
6.22
to see if a CPU Bayesian inverse exists. Furthermore, one may always compute the required pseudoinverses (provided they are not too large) to determine the formula for the Bayesian inverse.

Finally, we end with the verification that Theorem
6.22
reproduces the standard classical Bayes’ theorem as a corollary.

Example 6.33
.

Suppose
m_{x}=1
and
n_{y}=1
for all
(x,y)\in X\times Y
in Theorem
6.22
.
In this case,
F
can be viewed as a matrix of (linear transformations given by multiplication by) non-negative numbers. In this case,
F_{xy}^{*}=F_{xy}
as numbers.
If
q_{y}\neq 0
, then
Q_{y}=\mathds{1}_{n_{y}}
so that
\mathfrak{A}_{yx}=\frac{p_{x}F_{xy}}{q_{y}}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{yx}=0
, and
\mathfrak{C}_{yx}=0
. The two conditions in Theorem
6.22
are automatically satisfied.
Hence a Bayesian inverse exists. When
q_{y}\neq 0
, one sets

G_{yx}:=\frac{p_{x}F_{xy}}{q_{y}}
.
When
q_{y}=0
, the assignment in (
6.27
) defines
G_{yx}
as
G_{yx}:=\frac{1}{|X|}
. Thus, our proof of our quantum Bayes’ theorem reproduces classical Bayesian inference.

Appendix A
Permutations of the Choi matrix

Let

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a linear map.
Decompose the Choi matrix of
G
as
16
16

16

t=
top,
b=
bottom,
r=
right,
l=
left. The matrix rows and columns have been colored to illustrate how one slides them to obtain the permutation discussed afterwards.

\mathrm{Choi}(G)=\left[\begin{matrix}[0.91]\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\end{matrix}\!\begin{array}[]{>{\columncolor{red!30}}c>{\columncolor{blue!30}}cccc>{\columncolor{yellow!30}}c>{\columncolor{green!30}}c}G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}&\pagecolor{blue!30!red!30}{G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tr}}}&&\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\scriptsize$\bullet\bullet\bullet$}&&\pagecolor{yellow!30!red!30}{G\big(E_{1m}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}}&\pagecolor{green!30!red!30}{G(E_{1m}^{(m)})^{\mathrm{tr}}}\\
\pagecolor{red!30!blue!30}{G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}&&&&\pagecolor{yellow!30!blue!30}{G\big(E_{1m}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&\pagecolor{green!30!blue!30}{G\big(E_{1m}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}}\\
\makebox[0.0pt]{\hskip 63.0pt\Huge\vdots}&\makebox[0.0pt]{\hskip-62.0pt\Huge\vdots}&&&&\makebox[0.0pt]{\hskip 63.0pt\Huge\vdots}&\makebox[0.0pt]{\hskip-63.0pt\Huge\vdots}\\
\pagecolor{red!30!yellow!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}}&\pagecolor{blue!30!yellow!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tr}}}&&\raisebox{-6.0pt}{\scriptsize$\bullet\bullet\bullet$}&&G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}&\pagecolor{green!30!yellow!30}{G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tr}}}\\
\pagecolor{red!30!green!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&\pagecolor{blue!30!green!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}}&&&&\pagecolor{yellow!30!green!30}{G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}\\
\end{array}\!\right]

(A.1)

so that

G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)=\begin{bmatrix}G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}&G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tr}}\\
G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}&G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}\end{bmatrix}.

(A.2)

Note that the size of the square matrices
G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}
and
G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}
need not be the same, but we assume that the size of

G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}
equals the size of
G\big(E_{kl}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}
for all
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, and similarly for
G\big(E_{ij}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}
and
G\big(E_{kl}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}
.
Then, there exists a permutation matrix
U
such that

U\mathrm{Choi}(G)U^{{\dagger}}=\left[\begin{matrix}[0.91]\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\end{matrix}\!\begin{array}[]{>{\columncolor{red!30}}cc>{\columncolor{yellow!30}}c>{\columncolor{blue!30}}cc>{\columncolor{green!30}}c}G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}&\cdots&\pagecolor{yellow!30!red!30}{G\big(E_{1m}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}}&\pagecolor{blue!30!red!30}{G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tr}}}&\cdots&\pagecolor{green!30!red!30}{G(E_{1m}^{(m)})^{\mathrm{tr}}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\pagecolor{red!30!yellow!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}}&\cdots&G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tl}}&\pagecolor{blue!30!yellow!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tr}}}&\cdots&\pagecolor{green!30!yellow!30}{G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{tr}}}\\
\pagecolor{red!30!blue!30}{G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&\cdots&\pagecolor{yellow!30!blue!30}{G\big(E_{1m}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&G\big(E_{11}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}&\cdots&\pagecolor{green!30!blue!30}{G(E_{1m}^{(m)})^{\mathrm{br}}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\pagecolor{red!30!green!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&\cdots&\pagecolor{yellow!30!green!30}{G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{bl}}}&\pagecolor{blue!30!green!30}{G\big(E_{m1}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}}&\cdots&G\big(E_{mm}^{(m)}\big)^{\mathrm{br}}\\
\end{array}\!\right]

(A.3)

(which corresponds to the matrix
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\uline{\mathfrak{A}}&\uline{\mathfrak{B}}\\
\uline{\mathfrak{B}}^{{\dagger}}&\uline{\mathfrak{C}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
in the proof of Theorem
5.62
).
This is just a special case of what is discussed in Remark
5.55
, where the projection
P
is of the form
\mathds{1}_{m}\otimes\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\mathds{1}_{r}&0\\
0&0_{n-r}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
with
0\leq r\leq n
.
The permutation matrix
U
is the matrix associated to the permutation

\left(\begin{matrix}1&2&3&4&\cdots&2m-3&2m-2&2m-1&2m\\
1&m+1&2&m+2&\cdots&m-1&2m-1&m&2m\end{matrix}\right)

(A.4)

where
\begin{smallmatrix}i\\
j\end{smallmatrix}
means that the
i
-th row is sent to the
j
-th row.
Note that this is a permutation for the blocks of the appropriate sizes.
If
G(E_{ij}^{(m)})^{\mathrm{tl}}
has size
r\times r
, then
G(E_{ij}^{(m)})^{\mathrm{br}}
has size
(n-r)\times(n-r)
, and the general form of
U
is

U=\begin{bmatrix}\mathds{1}_{r}&0&0&0&&0&0\\
0&0&\mathds{1}_{r}&0&&0&0\\
&&&&\ddots&&\\
0&0&0&0&&\mathds{1}_{r}&0\\
0&\mathds{1}_{n-r}&0&0&&0&0\\
0&0&0&\mathds{1}_{n-r}&&0&0\\
&&&&\ddots&&\\
0&0&0&0&&0&\mathds{1}_{n-r}\end{bmatrix}.

(A.5)

For example, the matrix
U
that achieves this for (
5.87
) is

U=\begin{bmatrix}\mathds{1}_{2}&0&0&0\\
0&0&\mathds{1}_{2}&0\\
0&\mathds{1}_{1}&0&0\\
0&0&0&\mathds{1}_{1}\end{bmatrix}

(A.6)

and corresponds to the permutation

\left(\begin{matrix}1&2&3&4\\
1&3&2&4\end{matrix}\right),

(A.7)

which swaps the second and third blocks but leaves the first and fourth blocks alone.
